Application to the NSFG ~ Cycle I. The balanced half-sample replication
technique was used to estimate sampling variances for statistics produced
from NSFG Cycle I data. The variances were not computed for all statistics,
however, because the time and money needed to do so would have been
prohibitive. In addition, reports would be cumbersome if a variance
estimate were published for each statistic. Thus, variances for each type
of statistic were computed only for selected population subgroups which
were chosen to represent a wide variation in the size of the estimates.
Curves were then fitted to the relative standard error (RSE) estimates

(the ratio of the square root of the sampling variance to the statistic
itself), according to various models, and the curves were used to estimate
relative standard errors not directly calculated. Separate curves were
needed for black women and women of all other race classifications (women
of all races, women of races other than black, and white women) because
black women were oversampled. A statistic based on a given number of black
women, therefore, has a lower RSE than a statistic based on the same number
of women of all races.

A detailed description of the curve fitting procedures and of the models
used in these procedures can be found in other reports.!»!9 Equations
resulting from the curve fitting, which can be used to produce RSE
estimates for selected types of statistics calculated from Cycle I data,
are shown in Table L. RSE's for other types of statistics (e.g., life-
table probabilities) were computed individually; no curve fitting was
done.

Application to the NSFG - Cycle II. Sampling variances for statistics
produced from Cycle II data will also be calculated using the balanced
half-sample replication technique. The calculation of variances for
selected statistics and the subsequent curve fitting operations are
expected to be completed by the middle of 1979. In the interim,

sample design correction factors have been computed which allow the use
of Cycle I estimating equations to calculate provisional estimates for
Cycle II data (see Table M). The correction factors are necessary
because the Cycle II survey included a smaller sample of women and had
larger average cluster sizes than the Cycle I survey and thus has
larger sampling variances. Provisional standard errors for selected
aggregates and percents produced from Cycle II data are shown in

tables N and 0, respectively. Reports published by the National Center
for Health Statistics which discuss findings from Cycle II of the NSFG
include similar tables.

APPENDIX !

GLOSSARY OF TERMS

Conterminous United States.~The land area
consisting of the District of Columbia and all
States except Alaska and Hawaii.

Dwelling unit (DU).—A single room, or
group of rooms, that is intended for separate liv-
ing quarters. The people who live there must
live and eat separately from everyone else in the
building (or apartment) and the room or group
of rooms must have either

1. A separate entrance directly from the

outside of the building or through a
common hall, or

2. Complete kitchen facilities for the use
of this household only. Complete kit-
chen facilities include aél of the follow-
ing:

a. arange or cooking stove, and
b. asink with piped water, and
¢. uamechanical refrigerator.

Education.-The highest grade of school
completed.

Geographic region.—For the purpose of clas-
sifying the population by geographic area, the
U.S. Bureau of the Census has grouped the 50
States and the District vf Columbia into four
regions, as follows:

Region States included
Northeast......... Maine, New Hampshire, Ver-
mont, Massachusetts, Rhode
Island, Connecticut, New
York, New Jersey, Pennsyl-
vania

North Central... Michigan, Ohio, Indiana, Ili-
nois, Wisconsin, Minnesota,
Iowa, Missouri, North Da-
kota, South Dakota, Kansas,
Nebraska

Delaware, Maryland, District
of Columbia, Virginia, West
Virginia, North Carolina,
South Carolina, Georvia,
Florida, Kentucky, Texas,
Tennessee, Alabama, Missis-
sippi, Arkansas, Louisiana,
Oklahoma

Montana, Idaho, Wyoming,
Colorado, New Mexico, Ari-
zona, Utah, Nevada, Washing-
ton, Alaska, Oregon, Cali-
fornia, Hawaii

Alaska and Hawaii are not included in the NSFG
sample design.

Household.—A family living together, or five
or fewer unrelated individuals living together in
a DU.

Parity.—The number of live births a woman
has had.

Screener interview.—A preliminary interview
at the household to collect information about
the DU and to determine whether or not the
household includes one or more women who are
eligible for the detailed interview.

Standard metropolitan — statistical arca
(SMS.A).-A county or group of contiguous
counties (except in New England) which con-
tains at least one central city of 50,000 people
or more, or “twin cities” with a combined popu-
lation of at least 50,000. In addition, other con-
tiguous counties are included in an SMSA if,
according to certain criteria, they are socially
and economically integrated with the central
city.

Urban area.—As defined by the U.S. Bureau

44

of the Census, the urban areas of the United
States include all cities or “twin cities” with at
least 50,000 population in 1970 together with
the surrounding closely settled area and all
other incorporated or unincorporated popula-
tion centers with 2,500 inhabitants or more.

The next question is different. On this card (SHOW CARD) are two
different statements. Each may be answered with a simple "yes" or
"no." You choose one of the statements by tossing a penny. If the
penny turns up HEADS you just say "yes" or "no" to this statement
(POINT TO THE STATEMENT FOLLOWING THE HEAD OF THE PENNY). If the
penny comes up TAILS, then just answer "yes" or "no" to this statement
(POINT TO THE STATEMENT FOLLOWING THE TAIL OF THE PENNY).

Please do not tell me which side of the penny comes up. No one but
you - not even I - will know which statement was selected by the
penny toss.

the answer - "yes" or "no."

43

IF RESPONDENT WANTS YOU TO EXPLAIN THE QUESTION, SAY:

There are events which some: people would rather not talk about.
For instance, some people who have abortions would rather not talk
about them, even though they are legal.

But it is very important for doctors and public health services to
have a national count of the number of abortions each year. $0
scientists have developed this special way of asking questions.

Half the people we talk to will answer one statement and half will
answer the other, but we will not know which statement each individual
got. In this way, we are able to estimate the number of abortions

in the country, without knowing which individuals had them.

Of course, not everyone feels the same way about talking of abortions.
However, to make a reliable estimate for the country, we need everyone
to toss the penny and answer "yes" or "no" to the statement chosen by
the coin.

ASSURANCE OF CONFIDENTIALITY

In accordance with Section 308(d) of the Public Health Service (Act 42 USC 242m)
and the Privacy Act of 1974 (5 USC 552a), the National Center for Health
Statistics assures each respondent that all information which would permit
identification of any individual or family will be held in strict confidence,
will be used only by persons engaged in, and for purposes of, this study and
will not be disclosed to others for any purposes.

The next question is different. On this card (SHOW CARD) are two
different statements. Each may be answered with a simple "yes" or
"no". You choose one of the statements by tossing a penny. If the
penny turns up HEADS you just say "yes" or "no" to this statement
(POINT TO THE STATEMENT FOLLOWING THE HEAD OF THE PENNY). If the
penny comes up TAILS, Then just answer "yes" or "no" to this
statement (POINT TO THE STATEMENT FOLLOWING THE TAIL OF THE PENNY).

Please do not tell me which side of the penny comes up. No one
but you ~ not even I - will know which statement was selected by
the penny toss.

Here is a penny (RESPONDENT MAY USE HER OWN COIN). Just tell me

the answer - “yes" or "no."

RESPONDENT'S ANSWER WAS: YES. 6 ee ew ew we we ew eh eh ee UL a3
NO 2. 2 en ew ew ew ew ew ew we

IF RESPONDENT WANTS YOU TO EXPLAIN THE QUESTION, SAY:

There are events which some people would rather not talk about.
For instance, some people who have abortions would rather not talk
about them, even though they are legal.

But it is very important for doctors and public health services to
have a national count of the number of abortions each year. So
scientists have developed this special way of asking questions.

Half the people we talk to will answer one statement and half will
answer the other, but we will not know which statement each individual
got. In this way, we are able to estimate the number of abortions

in the country, without knowing which individuals had them.

Of course, not everyone feels the same way about talking of abortions.
However, to make a reliable estimate for the country, we need everyone
to toss the penny and answer "yes" or "no" to the statement chosen by
the coin.

Acknowledgement of Assistance

All manuscripts utilizing data made available through the
Consortium should acknowledge that fact as well as identify the
oxiginal collector of the data. The ICPSR Council urges all users of
the ICPSR data facilities to follow some adaptation of this statement
with parenthesis indicating items to be filled in appropriately or

deleted by the individual user.

The data (and tabulations) utilized in this
(publication) were made available (in part) by
the Inter-university Consortium for Political
and Secial Research. The data for NATIONAL
SURVEY OF FAMILY GROWTH, CYCLE II, 1976:
INTERVAL FILE were originally collected by the
National Center for Health Statistics.

Neither the collector of the original data nor
the Consortium bear any responsibility for the
analyses or interpretations presented here.

In order to provide funding agencies with essential information
about the use of archival resources and to facilitate the exchange of
information about ICPSR participants” research activities, each user of
the ICPSR data facilities is expected to send two copies of each
completed manuscript or thesis abstract to the Consortium. Please

indicate in the cover letter which data were used.
Study Description

The Survey of Family Growth was designed to provide data on
fertility, family planning, and related aspects of maternal and child
health. Fieldwork for Cycle II was carried out between January and
September of 1976. Interviews were conducted with a multistage
probability sample of women living in the coterminous United States,
aged 15-44 years inclusive, who were either currently married,
previously married, or never married but had offspring presently living
in the household. Approximately 33,000 households were screened to
identify the sample of women eligible for the survey.

The interval file contains detailed information collected from the
8,611 respondents about their pregnancy histories including the date,
outcome and order of all pregnancies, singie or multiple births, sex,
weight, residence, mortality, breastfeeding, and information on the
respondent”s periods of non-intercourse, contraceptive methods used and
regularity of use. The file consists of approximately 80 variables and
23,042 records, one record for each pregnancy reported by the sample
women. The record length is 200 characters.

Other information about the respondent may be found in the NATIONAL
SURVEY OF FAMILY GROWTH: COUPLE FILE (ICPSR 7902) released earlier.
The Couple File contains many summary measures of fertility derived
from the Interval File. ,

Processing Information

These data and documentation are distributed in the form received
by the ICPSR from the original collector. Thus, the ICPSR can take no
responsibility for the technical condition of the data nor for the
accuracy of the codebook.

Weetat, Inc., under contract to the National Center for Health Statistics.
Nome interviows vere conducted with 8,611 women 15-44 years of age who:

introduction

The National Survey of Family Growth, Cycle II was conducted in 1976
by Westat, Inc., under contract to the National Center for Health Statistics.
Home interviews were conducted with 8,611 women 15-44 years of age who were
married, previously married, or single with offspring in the household. The
data have been used by the National Center for Health Statistics as the basis
for a series of reports on the determinants and consequences of patterns of
family formation and fertility in the United States. To make the data avail-
able to a larger number of persons, ataundardized micro-data taupe transcripts
have been prepared for distribution. This Tape Documentation Manual describes
one such tape transcript; other items needed to use the tape transcript include
the reel of magnetic tape containing the transcript itself, a report on the
Sample Design, Estimation Procecures, Vartance Estimation for Cycle li of tie
survey, and the interview schedule. The complete packuge of tape and documenta-

tion may be obtained from the National Technical Information Service, Springfield,
Virginia 42161.

The "Interval File” described here contains all information collected from
respondents about their detailed pregnancy histories. Other information about
the respondent is in a separate "Respondent File" released earlier. The

Respondent File contains many summary measures ot fertility derived from che
Interval File.

Publications from the National Survey of Family Growth, Cycle IT

The National Center for Health Statistics has planned reports from the
NSFG, Cycle II on the following subjects: current contraceptive practice,
use of family planning services, wantedness of births, the relationship
of employment and fertility, sterilization, fecundity impairments and
fetal loss, and the use effectiveness of contraceptives.

A current listing of NSFG publications, and single copies of individual
publications, may be obtained free of charge by writing:

Editing of the Data

The data were manually edited in the field and in the home office of Westat,
Inc., the fieldwork contractor. Also, Westat conducted computer edits for
acceptable codes and internal consistency of the data, according to detailed
specifications prepared by NSFG staff. Following delivery of the data tapes
by Westat, NCHS independentiy replicated many of the consistency checks
according to the same specifications, and concluded that the “error rate”

was below the contract tolerance level of 1 percent. All “errors" discovered =
at any stage in editing were resolved. Thus, the data may be regarded as
very "clean" by most standards of irterview survey research. Neverthcless,
inconsistencies may appear in the data, either becduse the correct informztion
was not obtained and recorded, or discovered and corrected by editing, or
because the "logic" used in relating data items is faulty. For detailed
analyses requiring a high degree of consistency among variables, users may
wish to perform additional editing procedures.

Omitted Data Items

Certain items shown in the documentation have not been included in this data
tape. They may be identified in the documentation by the absence of a tape
position number opposite the deck and column numbers used in the raw data
file. Most deletions eliminated redundant identification repeated at the
beginning of each deck in the raw data file.
Missing Data

Some women selected for the sample were not interviewed because they
were not found at home, or they refused, or for some other reason the
interview could not be conducted. To adjust for this case nonresponse,
the final weight for respondents includes a ronresponse inflation factor;
that factor, which is discussed in the accompanying report on estima-
tion procedures, has the effect cf “over” counting respondents in
proportion to the rate vf nonresponse anong women like them in age,

race, and locality.

Among women who were interviewed, data are sometimes missing for
particular items, because the respondent would not or could not answer
the question, or because the interviewer did not ask the guestion.

For sone items, refusals and "dor'c know" responses contained useful
information, so they were coded, using "7" or "8". If it was not
useful to code those responses, or when the datum was missing due to
interviewer error or other reasons, the item was considered "not
ascertained", and coded "9", ,

For a few items, data not obtained in the interview were later imputed,
For instance, if the datum soughr was the month in which an event
occurred, but the respondent could remember only that it was "winter",
tne month was imputed as "January"; similarly, if the day of the month
was not known, it was imputed as the 15th. For a very few respondents,
race and age were also imputed, those data being necessary for post-
stratifying the sample, as discussed in the accompanying report on
estimating procedures, ; ae

With the few minor exceptions just discussed, missine dato were not
imputed in this file, This differs from Cycle I of the NSFC, in which
most missing data were imputed. The change was made to expedite
analysis and release of the data, but it does decrease the comparability
of the two Cycles. Users interested in comparing or combining the

two Cycles should consult tine NS*G sratf about this problen.
-7-

Inapplicable Questions

Not all questions were applicable to all respondents. If a question
was not applicable, the interviewer skipped to the next applicable
question. With certain exceptions noted in the tape documentation,
tape positions are blank for inapplicable questions. Some computer
programs read a blank as a nonnumeric character, but others read it
as zero. Users should take care to discinguish between blanxe and
zeroea in programs used with these date.

The meaning of statistics based on reponses to a question will depend,
of course, on who was asked the question and who was not. Because of
the rather complex skip patterns used in ine interview, it is often

not apparent who was asked a particular question. In the documentation,
however, the description of the inapplicable (blank) code category for
a question includes a precise listing of women not asked the question.
In addition to consulting that list, it is recommended that che sequence
of questionnaire items ancluding oa item of interest be examined care-

fully co determine the implications of skip patterns for interpreting
responses.

Sample Design, Estimation [rocedures, and Variance Estimation

A complete description of the sampling dcsiign, escimation procedures, and
variance estimation is included as ao separate item in the Tape Transcript
package. These matters are discussed here only as they pertain to Che tape
docunentation manual,

All analyses using these data should be “weighted;" that is, cach sample case
should be counted as if it were the number of women in the population it was
designed to represent. The number, the "weight" for each case, is shown in
positions 149-151.

Since the weighted statistics computed from these data are based on a sanple,
they will differ somewhat from the figures that would have been obtained if a
complete census had been taken using the same schedules, instructions, and
interviewing personnel and procedures. The variations that might occur by
chance because only a sample of the population is surveyed is measured by a
etatiatic called the "standard error," Standard errors of statistics from
complex samples, such ag that iu the NSFG, are computed by NCHS using the
“palanced half-sample replication method." That technique is described in
tre following publications:

National Center for flealth Statistics. "Replication: An
Approach to the Analysis of Data from Complex Surveys."

Vital and Health Statistics. FNS Pub. No. 1000-Sarles 2-No.
14.) Public Health Service, Washiaugton, D.C. U.S. Coverament
Printing Office, Apri’ 1966,

National Ceuter for Health Statistics. “Distribution and
Properties of Variance Estimators for Complex Multiscare
Probability Samples: An Fapirscal Distribution.” Vital

and Health Statistics. DHEW Pub. No. (HRA) 75-1339. Public
Health Service, Washington, D.C. U.S. Government Printing
Office, March 1975,

Procedures for computing standard errors of certain commen statistics, such
as agpregates, means, and proportions, are discussed in the accompanying
tTeport on variance estimation and in the “Technical Notes” to NCHS publica-
tions based on the NSFG,

The chances are about 68 out of 100 that an estimate from the sample would
differ from a complete census by less than Che Standard error. The chances
are about 95 out of 100 that the difference would be Tess than twice the
standard error and about 99 out of 100 that it would’ be Jess than 2.5 times
aa large.

Users are cautioned that tables of atandard errors based on assumptions of
simple random sampling (SRS) do net provide accurate estimates cf atandard
errors for the NSFG. On average, accurate estimates of standard errors for

the NSFG will be larger chan those estimated from 5RS5 tables, but the relation=-
ship of the two estimates is not known exactly, and may vary greatly from
statistic to statistic.

Date Coden c

During the interview, dates of events were obtained either as the year
alone, as wonth and year, or aa day, month, and year. For this file,
year and month were transformed into “century months" ty mrltiplying
the year by 12 and adding the number of the month, where January © 1,
February = 2, and so on, For instance, the century month code for
February 1959 ie (59 x 12) 4 2 = 710. The century month torm is more
convenient for compucing an ‘nterval between dates.

The century month codes from 30} through 972 are shown in the array
below, with the years from 1925 throuph 198 on the vertical axis

and the montha on the horizontal axis. The code for a given month

and year can be found by reading across the line for the appropriate
year to the column headed by the appropriate month. 4/ - Dates of events
expected to occur after 1994 are coded 995.

In cases where the question asked abour the date thar use of contraception
was terminated, but the respondent was still using a contraceptive, the
question was inapplicable, and the dace code was set equal co 996.

In cases where the question was inapplicavle for any other reason, the
position has three zerocr.

In cases where the yoar was refused, the date code was 997. In cases
where the year of the -vent was not known or not ascertained, tie
date code is 999. Jancary 1900 and earlier months are coded 001.

In cases where the month was not known, the first digit of the date

code was set equal to 9, and the last two digits were set equal to

the reperted year. For instance, if an cvent occurred in an unknown
month during 1968, the date code would be 968, the 9 indicating, thar the
month is unknown, and the 68 indicating the known year of the event.

Date codes in the range 901-972 require Special attention because of
their possible ambiguity. A code in that range could mean either the
unknown month of a year 190] through 1972, or a century month in 1975
through 198). UWusally the content of the quescion will fidicate
clearly which meaning is intended. Far instance, for questions about
events in the relatively distant past, such as the birthdate of the
husband, codes in the range 901-924 mean unknown months in the vears
1901-1924, not century months “in 1975 and 1976; but for questions about
relatively recent events, such a8 the last birth, ccdes in that range
mean century months in 1975 and 1976, not unknown months in che years
1901-1924, Or, codes in the range 925-972 could mean past events in
unknown months of the vears 1925-1972, or futuce events in reported
century months in 1977-1980; if the question is abour past events,
the iormer meaning. is intended, but che latter meaning is intended if
the question is about future events

i/ Events expected to occur 1981 through 1994 are coded vith a Y
in the firse digit followed by the last two digits of the year,
i.e., 1936 was coded 986.

Organization of wh. hata

The transcript consists of 23,042 fixed length records, one record for cach
Pregnancy reported by sample women. The length of cach record J: 200 positions.
As show in the folluwing Tape Layout, the first 152 positions coutain pregnancy
interval information and positions, 152-200 ure blank. The information about
the interval in positions l-151 is arranyed on the tape as it appeared in the
interview schedule, except that a few items have been omitted for reasons
discussed earlier. The arrangement is generally by topical areas; to improve
the flow of the interview, items un similar topics were sometimes separated.
Note also that some questions were skipped for some women, depending on their
answers to carlicr questions, and that slightly different forms of the interview
schedule were used for currently married and other wenen. The user should
examine the sequence of questions in which the item of interest occurs to
determine which. sample women were asked the question, and in which form the
question was asked.

Key to Documentation

The following pages document the contents of the tape transcript. The docurenta-
tfon is adapted from the documentation of the “raw” data file supplied by Westar,
Inc., the fieldwork contractor. The duta were oripinally keypunched in a deck
and column format, and the deck and column numbers have been retained in this
decumentation; the deck number is shown on cach page, and the coiumn numbers

are shown next to cach data deseription entry. The duck and column number:

also appear in the: tnterview sehcdule, and thus provide a useful cross refcoreace
‘Setween the two documents,

Cross reference between the interview schedule and this documentation js also
Possible using question numbers, which are inc}uded in this documentation besdda
data description entries, and are, of course, in rhe interview schedule. Each
data description entry also includes a description of the interview schedule
source of the data, usually the actual question asked ef the respondent.

Zn addition to describing the source of the data, the data deseription entry
describes cach numerical catcsory into vhich responses were coded. The catepory
deseriplions are intended to be self-explanatory, but some details of coding
Procedurcs are omitted because they are volundnous and affect few cases.

The tape position nurber shows the physical location of the datum on the tape
transcript, lf no tape position number is shown, the datum has been omitted
from the transcript, for reasons sliscussed above.

In the left morgin heside cach data description entry are shown the numbers of
fatervals in cach category ef the item. seporately for black women, white women,
and waren of all vaces combined. These maryinal distributions are unvedphtled
counts of sample conen; they are not estimates of numbers or distribution for
the populanioen represented by the sarple, To make such estimates, sample cases
Must be waishted by the final weaght (post-stracifieation weight), as discussed
above ond in Che accompanying report on est foating precedurces. The naked phted
wfarginal distributions are provided here only as a guide tea the aualyse, who
often wants Co hnow in advance if the number of cases im a particular catesory
is sufficiently larpe to sustain 3 particular type of analysis.

INTRODUCTION

The primary mission of the National Center for Health Statistics (NCi15)

is to collect and publish data relating to the health of the popularion

of the Unired States. In carrying out this mission, NCHS collects data

on vital events registered in the United States, conducts inventories

of health facilities and manpower, and conducts probability sample

surveys based on household interviews, health examinations, and medical
records. Data collection programs are supplemented by research projects
which investipace new techniques of data coilection and evaluate currently
operating programs.

In response to the need fe~ current information on the interrelated topics
of fertility, faaily plann..sm, and their effects on population growth,

the Rational Survey of Family Growth (NSFG) was established as an inregral
part of the NCHS program in 1972. The purpose of the survey is to collect
data relating to natality and the process of family formation and dissolu-
tion; therefore, it was placed in the Division of Vital Stacistics. The
NSFG was designed as a cyclic survey; that is, data are to be collected
every few years by means of a sample survey. The first cycle of the
survey was conducted in 1973, the secoad in 1976, and Cycle IZI will be
conducted in 1960. ~

The tarset population of beth Cycle I and Cycle II of the NSFG consisted
of the civilian houschold population of women 15-44 years of age and
living in che conrerminous United States who were currently married,
previously married, or single mothers with offspring of their own living
in che household at the time of interview. Data were collected by means
of personal interviews of about an hour in length with a probabiliry
sample of these wenen. The interviews provided information on trends and
differentials in fertility, family planning practices, sources of family
planning advice and services, the use and effectiveness of contraceptives,
and aspects of maternal and child heal.h that are clicsely related to
fnzily planning.

The sample design and data collection for Cycle II of the NSFC were
contractec to “estat, Inc., of Rockville, Marvland. The sample consisted
of 10,292 eligible women, of whom 8,611 (84.4 percent) completed extended
interviews. All interviews were conducted between January and September
of 1976. The sanple design employed cto select these woneu is described
in detail in this report. Also described are the technique used to
estimate population parameters and the procedures used to obtain
provisional sanpling veriances.

DESIGN SPECIFICATIONS
The development of un efficient sample design must take into account the
primary survey objectives, the azount of funds available, logistical
problems, time limitations, estinates of population characteristics and
distribution, and operating costs. These requirements dictated a
stracified aulcistage probability sumple design for Cycle 11 of the NSFG,
based essentially on the following set of specifications: _

l. The target population was defined to be the civilian household
population of women living in the centerminous United States
who were l5-s4 years of age and either currently marricd,
previously married, or single mothers with one or more children
born to them currencly living in the houschold.

2. The sample would consist of approximately 10,000 woren selected
from an initial probability sample of households. It would
include about 4,J00 black women and 6,000 white women anc women
of other races. Trained field staff were to conduct a screening
interview with a responsible member of esch sample houschold to
determine if there were any eligible women (the screener
questionnaire is reproduced in Appendix II}. When a household
contained one eligible woman, sne was included in the sample.
In households with more chan one eligible woman, the staff ‘
member would randomly select one woman for the sample.

3. Data were to be collected from the sample vouen by teans of
personal interviews lasting an average of one hour.

4. All interviewers were required to be female.

5« The interviewer would collece information on fereility, fanily
planning practices, sources of family planning services, and
telated maternal and child hearth practices.

6. The field work would be completed in approximately six months.

7. The target interview rate for the total sample and both major
subsamples by ztace was 90 percent of the expected number of
women from cll sample households (i.e., screener and interview
nonresponse comvined weuld ideally be no more than 10 percent),

&. The contrector would design and implement procedures io measure
and control the quality of data collection and data preparation.

- SAMPLE DESIGN
Summary
The sample design for Cycle Il of the Naclonal Survey of Funily Growrh
(NSFG) was a five-stase probability design which incorporated a
ah athe te gered

supplementary sample of new (post-1969) housing units. The counties and
independent citics that comprise che total land area of the conrerminous
United States were combined to form a frame of primary sampling units
(PSUs). During the first stage of the sampling process, which involved
extensive stratification, 79 primary sampling units were chosen from
this frame. Census enumeration districts (EDs) vere then identified for
each of the selected primary sampling unirs, and, during the second
Stage, these enumeration districts were stratified according to the
percent of their population that was black and a systematic sample was
drawn. The rate at which enumeration districts were sampled raried by
Becond-stage strata to ensure the current racial comnosition of the
€inal sample of women. ,

The chird stage required the identification of area segments (groups of
houses) within sample enumeration districts and the random selection of
one segment from each district. All sampie dvelling units (DUs) built
prior to 1970 were sclected in this way. However, seguents of new
construction units (post=-1969) were drawn from a supplementary sample of
building permits selected from building pernict offices in the 79 primary
Bappling units chosen during the firse stage of the sampling process.

The fourth stage resulted in rhe selection of households within sample
segments. In segments from enumeration districts with a l0-percent or
greacer black population, this process required the random sclection and
identification of 13 out of 20 dwelling units. White households from
rhese scements were included in the sample only if they were found in

the subsampled units. However, al] dSlack households in these segzuents,
aod all households (regardless of race) in the remaining seguents were
included in the sample. At each sample houschold an interviewer attempted
to complete a Household Screener and identify women eligible for interview.
When core than one pocercial .espondent was found during this fifcth-stage
operation, all eligible women in the household were Listed and one was
Tandonly «elected.

THE FIRSi-STACE SELECTION OF PSUs ~

The Sampline frare, The counties and independent cities of the conter=
minous United States have been crouped into about 1,900 primary sarnling
units (PSU's) by the Sureau of rhe Census for its Currene Population
Survey. These Census PSUs were used by Westat, Inc., with minor
modificatiers, as the sampling frame for the NSFG. Each PSU consisted

of an individual ceunty or grouping of counties and where Standard Metro-
politan Stitistical Accas (SMSAs) wer2 defined) they were used as PSUs.
Seventy-nine PSUs were sciected in the first stage of the sampling process.

'sMsa definicions usca are those of 1970. No attempt was made to have
the sample reflect new SMSAS created since 1970 or changes in SNSA
definitions since that date. In New Englund, State Economic areas (SEAS)
were used instead of SNSAs so that PSUs would in all cases be defined

as groups of complete countics.

The sample contains 25 self-representing (included with certainty) PSUs
comprising 18 separate SNSAs. These include the 14 largest SMSAs, with
’ ¥970 populations of more than 1,850,000, and 4 sliphtly smaller ones
which were made self-representing because they could not easily be placed :
in strata with other PSUs. Four of the largest SNSAs, which contained :
several counties each, were subdivided into smaller PSU's with an average
population of about 2,500,000.

The Selecrion of Nonself=-Revresenrine PSUs. Prior to the selecrion of. ‘
the remaining PSUs, all nonseli-representing (probability of selection
_ was less than unity) PSUs were grouped into 35 strata of approximately

equal size. Nineteen strata contained SMSAs and 16 contained non-S4SA
areas. .

The more than 200 nonself-representing SMSAs were sorted into 19 strata
of about 4,000,000 persons each, In defining the appropriate stratum,
four characteristics were considered. In order of priority, these
characteristics are: :
1. Region of the country (see Table A)
2. Percent change in population between 1960 and 1970
3. Percent of the population employed in manufscturing
4. <A Socloeconomic Index (developed by Westat, Inc.) which is
based on: percene of the population that is white; percence
of households which either lack plumbing or are overcrowded;
and the dependency ratio.‘
The nearly 1,709 renaining PSUs were then sorced into an additional 1€ '
Strata. The erireria in order of priority for this stratificacion are :
as follows: ; . ‘
1. Region of the country
2. Percent of the population living in urban areas
3. Percent change in population between 1960 and 1570
4. The Westat Soctoenoneaie Index.
This stratification tnsured the prepoctionste representation of women

by region, residence, race, and socioeconomic -status, and also reduced
sampling error.

“The dependency racic is the catio of the number of people who are less
than 1S or more than 35 vears of age, to the number of people sho are
hetween the ages 13 and 64 inclusive.

Following the stratification process, PSUs were selected in two stages.?
In the firse stage one PSU was sniected from each of the 3$ strata with
a@ probability proportionate to size. The second stage selection of the
remaining 19 PSUs was Chen accomplished in three steps. First the 35
strata were combined fato 19 superstraca. This recombination was cone
in such a way as to produce strata which were, as far as possible,
homogencous with respect to region, metropolitan composition (SMSA vs.
non-SMSA), rate of population change between 1949 and 1970, and the
percent of the population chat was black. Four of the resultant
superstrata contained a single srratum each, 14 contained 2 str3ta,

one cont2ined 3 strata. The second step was the selection, with a
probability proportionate co size, of a single stratum within each
superstratuu. The final step vas the selection, also with a probcbility
proportionase to stze, of one PSY frea edch of che 19 straca. Since

the second stage selection of 19 PSUs vas done independently of the
firse scape selection of 35 PSUs (f1.¢., sampling was done with replace-
ment) it vas possible fer PSUs to be selected twice; this was the case
for ome PSU. All selected PSUs are shown in Table B, and a ditagran of
the first stage of the sampling process is shown in Figure 1.

THE SECOND STAGE SELECTION OF ENUMIRATION DISTRICTS

Sampling Rates. Because the final sasple of women for the National
Survey or Famtiy Crowth was intended te consise of approximarely 4,0C0
black women and 6,000 women of other races, different sampling rates
were required for the cwo groups. Given a 100 percent resoense rate,
the approximate sampling fraction needed to produce the requived number
of black wonen was 1 in 950, while the corresponding fracticn for women
of other races wss about 1 in 4,610 (see Table C). The firse step in
producing these disparate rates occurred in the second stage of che
Sampling procesa: the selection of 1970 Census enumerd.ion districts
(EDs) within sample PSUs. This was accomplished b.° srracifytag =Ds

by the percenc of the population that was black, and by using a

hisher rate of selection in the strata with a 10 percent or greater
black population.

The Strorification of Ensseracion Piseriers. sefore the EDs were
stratiticd a cereain arount of recendbination was neecssary. Although
the block grougs (stall EDs used by the Census in densely populated
areas) and EDs averagcd about 400 housing units in size, there vas
considerable tariaticn about this mean. Thus the smallest EDs and
block groups were combined to form £9s which contained 10 or more
housing units cach.

the decision to change to a 79 PSU azsign. Ic was thus nore efficient
ta go through a second stage of seleccing additional PSUs than to
select 2 new independent sampic. .

The stratification of EDs was accomplished on che oasis of information
included in the 1970 Census of Population and Housing. These data were
used to determine the proportion of the population of each ED that was
black, und thus to which stratum it should be assigned. The strata were
defined as follows:

Stratum 1: EDs with a black population of 59.5 percent or greater
Stratum 2: EDs with a black pepulation of 29.5 to 59.499 percent
Stratum 3: EDs with a black population of 9.5 to 29.499 percent

Stratum 4: EDs with a black population of 0.0 to 9.499 percent.
Devzerninazion of Size. After chis initial stratification by race, each
CD was ussigned a measure of size which was the number of area segments
(groups of vear-round housing unites} chat it containcd. The size of each
area segment, in curn, was a funcefion of TD stratum, ' The avernge number
of housing unics per segnent was 15 in strata 1 and 4, 30 in stratum 2,
and 60 in stratum 3, The measure of size for EDs in secrata 1 and 4,
for example, was chus the number of year-round housing units they con-
tained divided by 15. This ratio was then. reunded to che nearest inteser
for sampling purposes. ;

If an ED assigned to stratum 2 or 3'did not have a number of housing
units equal to at least two thirds of the average segnont size for that
stratum, a second combining operation was performed. The newly created
EDs were then placed in the proper stratum and a new measure af size
computed. In both the first and second cosbining operacions, two
restrictions applied: no more than 4 EDs were to be grouped together;
and combined EDs must all be from the same county.

Sequencing of the EDs. Following the assignments cf a measure of size,
aleEDs were sorted by: (1) PSU number; (2) stratum number; (3) the
Westac Economie Index.’' The sorting by PS.’ number had the effect of
grouping EDs by region and by type of PSU (self-representing SMsAs,
nonself-rcprasenting SMSAs, and non-SMSA arcas) within region. The
Westat Economic Lndex was sequenced within strata in ascending order

in the firse and third strata, and in desecnding order in the second
an¢d fourth strata. The sequencing operation combined with the
systematic sampling of EDs which was carried over from one ?SU to the
next and from one stratus co the nexc, preduced the effective straci-
fication of EDs hy region, residence, and income. This stratification
was intended to achicve a small reduction in sample vartance and ensured
a representative sample of women by these variables.

"The Census ED file contains only information collected an a 109 pereen:
basis and thus does netcincludy income data. Westar, therefore, Lapured
a median income for each ED by creating a renression of income on such
items as value of home and rent paid, and appliving the regression model
to cach ED. These iuputed values form the Vestac Economie Index.

The Selection of EDs. Prior to the sampling operation, the measure of
size for cach ED was inflated by the inverse of the probability of
selection for the PSU chat it was in. This weighting has two effects:
households from all PSUs have exactly the same overall prebability of
being included in the final sample; and a representative sample can be
achieved while using the same sampling fraction across all PSUs.

Using a random start, a systematic sample of EDs was drawn. The sampling
interval was 760 segments in strata 1 through 3 and 4,256 segments in
stratum 4. Thus an ED was selected if the sum of the random start plus
the accumulation of intervals fell within it. <A few EDs which had an
inflated measure of size that was greater than the sampling interval

were selected more than orce. -- ‘

Only one random start was needed. The systematic sampling was carried
over from oe stratum to che next within PSUs, and from the last ED
Selected in one PSU to the First ED in the next.

THE FOURTH STAGE SELECTION OF HOUSEHOLDS

Suncampling for Race. During tha second stage of the sampling process

a hishes sampling rate was used for the selection of enumeration districts
in strata 1 through 3 than for those in strata 4 and 5. The higher rates
in strate 1 throurh 3 (EDs where 10 percent or more of the population was
black} was intended to produce a higher probability of selection for
black women than for women of other races and thus yield the approxi-
mately 4.099 sample black women (out of a total sarcple of- about 10,000)
required by the NSTG design specifications. However, since abour 36
percent of eligible wouen {a those strata were expected to be of races
other chan black, this procedure alone would also have praduced an
unnecessarily large sample of white women and women of other races.
Consequently, the fourth stage selection of sanple househutdsa involved

a subsanpling cperstion designed to reduce the overall sampling rate

for these women and produce a sumple of the appropriate size.

This subsanpling operation included the use of a computer to designate
a portion of sanple cwelling units in those strata as subsample units.
All houscholds containing eligible black women Secame sanple houscholds
but houschoids containing eligible women cf other races were tnueluded
in the sample only if they were found in designated subsample units.
The interviewers received instructions on the Household Screeners as to
which househoids were to include any eligible woman and in which ones
only black women were to be included.

ple initial sample design called for the subsampling of househslds in
strata 1 through 3 at the rate of 1 in 5. This rate would have produced
approximately equal sampling rates for white women and women of other
races across 412 5 strata; 1 in 3,800 in scrata 1 throurh 3 and 1 in
“ 4,256 in strata 4 and 5. However, after che field work was well under-
way aad the 1 in 5 subsample already selectod, a decision was made co
increase the subsanpling rate to 13 in 20. This decision was necessary
becouse sinnificentiy fever than expected of these women were seins
incluged in the Ctnal sample. This undercoverage resulced primarily
from two factors: fever than expected DUa in strata 1 through «
(see table DP); and a ponulacion shift whieh produced fever than eox-
peeted elicthle white women and women of other races in strata lb cheough 3.

{a 1 in S subsampie), the inereased subsamoling rare was achieved

NON-RESPONSE FOLLOWUP

_ The Subsampling of Non-Response Cass. An elite corms of traveling

interviewers and assistant supervisors was assigned the task of
obtaining interviews a sampie households included in che followup
effort. Households were assigned to this operation after all efforts
to complete interviews were cxhausted by local interviewers. Non-

-Tesponse cases that appeared to be non~convertible refusals were

excluded, however.

Prior to the interviewing effort a 50 percent subsample of these cases
was selected using systematic cluscer sampling procedures, This sub-
samplinz, designed to reduce interviewing costs, was accomplished in

two ways. In iarge-city PSU's, where large numbors of non-response
cases were expected, the housvholds were grouped by segment, the
Segments were sequences in descending order by the number of followup
cases they contained, and systematic sample of one-half of the segmenrs’
was drawn. For the remaining PSUs, non-response households were grouped
by PSU, the PSUs were sequenced in descending order by the number of
followup cases they contained, und a 50 percent sample of PSUs selected.
Approxinacely four and one~half percent of the respondents who vere
included in the final sample came tn 4s a result of this followup
effort. They represent about 9 percent of the pepulacion of respondents
(since they were subsampled at the rate of 1 im 2) and thus are weighted
double relative to other women in che sample (see table F for the percents
of women sclected at various sampling rates).

CHARACTERISTICS OF THE SAMPLE

Response Rates. The first four stages of the sample design {plus the
missed DU procedure) resulted in the identification of 32,652 semsic

BUs5, However, during the fifth szage screening operacion interv‘evers
discovered that 5,590 of these were either vacanc or were nor Dis as

Samole Stze, The 8,611 women included in the final eample is nearly
14 percent below the desired sample size of 10,000, Further, this
undersampling is greater among bluck women than among women cf other
faces; the number of sample black women was ebout 25 percent below the
expected cocal compared to an about 9 percent undersemple of women of
ether races. However, much of this racial differential is due to the
decision, made after data collection was well underway, tn inerease
the subsampling rate for nou-black women in struta 1 through 3, thus

: : increasing the overall sampling rate for these women and reducing the
extent to which they were under represented,

The smaller than expected sample is duc to a number of factors, the most
: _ important of which is the subsampling of non-response followup cases.

' Since fifty perceat subsampling was used for these cases, all women

: brought into the sample as a result of the foll «up effort actually

. represent two non-response cases. Allowing these women to assume a

: double weight, the number of women in the sample hecomes 8,996 (nearly
90 percent of the expected cotal}, of which 3,149 are black woucn
(about 79 percent of the expected total) and $,847 are women of other
races (about 97 percent of the expected total).

Other important factors are:
1. Nigher than expected non-response rates. The expected combined

response rate was 490 percent compared to an actual rate of 82.7
percenc.

2. Fewer than exnectad dwellinz units in some strats. This factor
may have teen the result of higher than expected rates of
N abandonment and demolition of structures in some areas (sce
table Db). ;

3. Missed eligible women within some DUs.

4. Pooulation movement across strata. This problem is especially
eriticai for black women sinecy chey were sampled at a much
different rate in straca 1 through 3 chan in strata 4 and 5.

Ie is estimated that about 670,000 black wemen had moved from
areas where Chey would have been sumpled at the rete of 1 in
76) to ateas where they were sampled at the rate of 1 in 4,256.
Thus this factor alone could nave reduced the sample of black
women by about 725, .

ESTIMATION

Welghtin: Procedures. Since the NSPG is designed to produce unbdlased
estinates for the entire ponularton of eligible wenen in the Uni.ed States,
the sample data muse be fiallated to the level ef the population ‘rom whicit
the satple was drawn. The inflation factor, or weight, for vach woman ‘ts
the predkce of sevaral adfustuents, one or more for cach stage of sampling.

ey TOT eee

2 a ; . f F a 4 J: -
28

Three types of adjustments are involved: inflation by the reciprocal of
the probabllities of selection; non-response adjustment; postratification
adjusctmenc,

Infiation by the Recinrocal of the Probabilities of Selecticn. The weight
for each woman is the product of the reciprocals of che probabilities of
selecting (1) the PSU, (2) the ED, (3) the segment, (4) the household,

and (5) the eligible sample person.

Non-Response Adiustment. Each sample weight is adjusted for non-response
to the ilecusehold Screener (screener non-response) and non-response of
sample women to the detailed NSFG quescionnaire (interview non-response).
These adjustments are necessary becausé the phenomenon of non-response

introduces bias into any probability sample survey. The vespondents

to a survey may have a much difference distribution of demographic or
health characteristics than the non-respondents. Even if the distribu-
tion of demegrashic and health characteristics is about the same for
respondents anc non-respondents, the two groups are by definition
different bacause the respoudents participated in the survey while

the non-responderts did not. Non-response adjustments minimize the
impact of non-response bias on the final estimates by imputing to
non-responding households and women the characteristics of "similar"
respondents. Similar respondents were judged co be households in the
same PSU and stratum, and women in the same age-race class and PSU.

Poststrarification by. Age and Race. The weight for each ever married
respondent is multiplied by a poststratificarion adjustment fzeccor

that is determined by the woman's age and race. The 12 adjustment
facters shown in table J make NSFG estimates of ever married voren in
each age-race class equal to tadependent control totals for April 1976,
the approximate midpoint of data collection. The control totals are
based on data from the Current Population Survey (CPS) conducted by the
U. S. Bureau of the Census. No poststritificacion adjustment is applied
to the weights for single mothers because reliable control totals are
fer available. Poststratification achieves such of the improvement in
precision that would have been attained if the sample had been drawn
fren a populacion stratified by age and race. A discussion of the
method used to compute the CPS control totals and a comparison of simple
iaflatton and poststratification estimators can be found in another
veporc’.

The balawed halt-sample replication technique described in detail in
other NOMS > ports ©” is usea to estimate NSFG variances. AN emririca!
studs bys Bean “ eives evicence that the half-sarple technique produces
highiy reliable, essentially unbiased variance estimates.

There cre three isportant practical reasons why half~sample replication

is bei.g used: -

1. Pregror.sing difficulties are reduced because halfesample variances
wted by taking a simple average of squared deviations of
half-sisple estizates from the estimate based on the full sample.
Instead of having to program an exceedingly difficult variance
formula, the programmer nuse simply adjust the estimation formuls
fo cotpute cstinstes from appropriately chosen half-samples.

2. The cesplere Jlgebraic formula for NSFG variances is unknown be-
cause of the complexity of the desian. Although algebraic
expressions can be derived for particular subprocedures = such
@s the individual stages of sampli.g and the poscstratificstion
and ronresponse adjustments = 2 single, exact variance equation
has not been developed.

3. As states by McCarthy 2,0 “Wariance estizates based upon the
replicated estimates will mirvor the eflects of all aspects of
samplinn and estimation that are permitted to vary candomly
from replicate to replicate.‘ also, replicated Salf-sampic
variances inchude some of the ve riabilise due co nonsamplin,
(muasure.ent) error, as well a+ sampling variability.

by selecting one of che paired sample PSU's’ from each stratum. If che

Because it is impractical to compute the }{ for the entire set cf 2!
possible half-saroles when 4 is large, a subset of hatf-samples is
selecced to produce the estimates. A set of side conditions relating
to the selection cf PSU's for the halr-samples has been develcut. by
McCarthy 2:3 based on work by Plackert and Surcan 3 and Gurnev.?
These side conditions greacly increase the stability of he by
eliminating a between-strata component of variance that is otherwise
present. The value of ste obtained from a subsez of half-samples ths:
ave chosen according to the McCarthy criteria is equal to the valuc that
would be obtuined using all 9+ half-samples. 4 sez of half-sampies that
satisfy the McCarthy criteria is called a “balanced set," and the pro-
cedure is reterred to as “balanced half{-sample replication."

Appiication to rhe NSFG - Cycle 1. The balanced half-sample replication
technique was used to estimate sampling variances for etatistics produced
from NSFG Cycle I cata. The variances were not computed ror all statistics,
however, because the time and money needed to do so would have been
prohibitive, In addition, reports would be cumbersome if a variance
eStinate were published for each statistic. Thus, variances for each type
of statistic were computed only for selected populaticn subgroups which
were chosen to represent a wide variation in the size of the estinates,
Curves were then fitted to the relative standard error (RSE) ercimates

(the racio of the square root of the sampling variance to the statistic
“itself), according to various models, and the curves were used to estimate
relative standard errors not directly calculated, Separate curves were
needed for black wesen and women of all. other race classifications (women
of all races, women of races other than black, and white women) because
black women vere oversazpled. A statistic based on a given number of black
women, therefore, has a lower RSE than a statistic based on the same number

of women of ail races,

A derailed ceseription of the curve fitring procedures and of che models
uscd in chese procedures can be found in other reports, }0t0 Equations
resulting from the curve fittine, which can be used to produce RSE
estimates for selected types of statistics calculated from Cycle 1 data,
are shown in Table &. RSE’s for other types of staristics (e.g., life-
table probabilities) were computed individually; no curve fitting was
donc.

Apolication to the NSFC = Cycle II. Sampling variances for statistics
produced from Cycle il data will also be calculated using the balanced
halfesanple replication technique. The calculation of variances for
selectcd statistics and uhe subsequent curve fitting operacions are
expected to be completed by the middle of 1979. In the interin,

sample design correction factors have becn computed which allow the use
oc Cycle 1 estimating equations to calculate provisional estimates for
Cycle I] data (sec Table M). The corre tion factors are necessary
because the Cycle IT survey ineluded a smaller sample of women and had
larser average cluster sizes than the Cycle © survey and thus has
latger sampling variances. Provisional standard errors for selected
aggresatzs and percents produced from Cycle TI data are shown in

tables N and 0, respectively. Reports published by the National Center
for Health Statistics which discuss findings from Cycle II of the NSFG
incluce similar tables.

ASSURANCE OF CONFIDENTIALITY

In accordance with Section 308(d) of the Public Health Service Act (2 USC 242m)
and the Privacy Act of 1974 (5 USC 552a), the National Center for Health
Statistics assures each respondent that al] information which would permit
identification of any individual or family will be held in strict confidence,
will be used-only by persons engaged in, and for purposes of, this study and
will not be disclosed to others for any purposes.

CARD

The next question is different. On this card (SHOW CARD) are two
different statements. Each may be answered with a simple "yes" or
"no.“ You choose one of the statements by tossing a penny. If the
penny turns up HEADS you just say "yes" or "no" to this statement
(POINT TO THE STATEMENT FOLLOWING THE HEAD OF THE PENNY). If the
penny comes up TAILS, then just answer "yes" or "no" to this statement
(POINT TO THE STATEMENT FOLLOWING THE TAIL OF THE PENNY).

Please do not tell me which side of the penny comes up. No one but
you - not even I - will know which statement was selected by the
penny toss.

Here is a penny (RESPONDENT MAY USE HER OWN COIN). Just tell me
the answer - "yes" or "no."

RESPONDENT'S ANSWER WAS: YES. 2 ew ew ew ee we ee el el CUD 43

1 Co a

iF RESPONDENT WANTS YOU TO EXPLAIN THE QUESTION, SAY:

There are events which some: people would rather not talk about.
For instance, some people who have abortions would rather not talk
about them, even though they are legal.

But it is very important for doctors and public health services to
have a national count of the number of abortions each year. So
scientists have developed this special way of asking questions.

Half the people we talk to will answer one statement and half will
answer the other, but we will not know which statement each individual
got. In this way, we are able to estimate the number of abortions

in the country, without knowing which individuals had them.

Of course, not everyone feels the same way about talking of abortions.
However, to make a reliable estimate for the country, we need everyone
to toss the penny and answer "yes" or "no" to the statement chosen by
the coin.

The next question is different. On this card (SHOW CARD) are two
different statements. Each may be answered with a simple "yes" or
"no". You choose one of the statements by tossing a penny. If the
penny turns up HEADS you just say "yes" or "no" to this statement
(POINT TO THE STATEMENT FOLLOWING THE HEAD OF THE PENNY). If the
penny comes up TAILS, Then just answer "yes" or "no" to this
statement (POINT TO THE STATEMENT FOLLOWING THE TAIL OF THE PENNY).

Please do not tell me which side of the penny comes up. No one
but you ~- not even I - will know which statement was selected by
the penny toss.

Here is a penny (RESPONDENT MAY USE HER OWN COIN). Just tell me
the answer - "yes" or "no."

RESPONDENT'S ANSWER WAS: Yes... ee ee ee ee eee 43

NO 2. 2 e 2 e © © © ew ew ew ew

IF RESPONDENT WANTS YOU TO EXPLAIN THE QUESTION, SAY:

There are events which some people would rather not talk about.
For instance, some people who have abortions would rather not talk
about them, even though they are legal.

But it is very important for doctors and public health services to
have a national count of the number of abortions each year. So
scientists have developed this special way of asking questions.

Half the people we talk to will answer one statement and half will
answer the other, but we will not know which statement each individual
got. In this way, we are able to estimate the number of abortions

in the country, without knowing which individuals had them.

Of course, not everyone feels the same way about talking of abortions.
However, to make a reliable estimate for the country, we need everyone
to toss the penny and answer "yes" or "no" to the statement chosen by
the coin.
